Local-global principle and integral Tate conjecture for certain varieties
Zhiyu Tian

Abstract
. We give a geometric criterion to check the validity of the integral Tate conjecture for one-cycles on a smooth projective variety that is separably rationally connected in codimension one, and to check that the Brauer-Manin obstruction is the only obstruction to the local-global principle for zero-cycles on a separably rationally connected variety defined over a global function field. We prove that the Brauer-Manin obstruction is the only obstruction to the local-global principle for zero-cycles on all geometrically rational surfaces defined over a global function field, and to the Hasse principle for rational points on del Pezzo surfaces of degree four defined over a global function field of odd characteristic. Along the way, we also prove some results about the space of one-cycles on a smooth projective variety that is separably rationally connected in codimension one, which leads to the equality of the coniveau filtration and the strong coniveau filtration on degree 3 3 homology of such varieties.



1.
Introduction

In this paper, we adopt the following conventions.
A variety over a field
k
is a finite type, integral, separated
k
-scheme.
Given a variety
V
defined over a field
k
,
we use
\overline{V}
to denote the base change of
V
to a pre-fixed algebraic closure of
k
.
An algebraic set over a field
k
is a finite type, geometrically reduced, separated
k
-scheme.
By a point in a
k
-scheme, we mean a closed point, unless otherwise specified.
A pointed
k
-scheme is a
k
-scheme
X
together with a finite number of
k
-rational points
(X,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
. A morphism between pointed schemes
f:(X,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\to(Y,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})
is a morphism of schemes
f:X\to Y
such that
f(x_{i})=y_{i},i=1,\ldots,n
.

For any abelian group
A
, any integer
m
, and any prime number
\ell
, we use
A[m]
to denote the group of
m
-torsions in
A
,
A/m
to denote the quotient
A/mA
, and
A\hat{\otimes}\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}
to denote the inverse limit
\lim\limits_{\xleftarrow[n]{}}A/\ell^{n}
.

1.1.
The Local-global principle for zero-cycles

Given a smooth projective variety defined over a global field, a natural and important problem is to find criteria for the existence of rational points and a description of the set of all rational points.
The Hasse principle and the weak approximation problem, or the local-global principle, gives a characterization of this set in terms of the adelic points.
There are various obstructions for the local-global principle to hold, notably the so called Brauer-Manin obstruction. A conjecture due to Colliot-Thélène states that for rationally connected varieties defined over a global field, this is the only obstruction.
The study of zero-cycles, as natural generalizations of rational points, has also drawn a lot of attentions in recent years.
Motivated by the case of rational points,
Colliot-Thélène has formulated the following conjectures on the local-global principle for zero-cycles.

Conjecture 1.1
.

[
CT99
, Conjecture 2.2]

Let
X
be a smooth projective geometrically integral variety defined over the function field
\mathbb{F}_{q}(B)
of a smooth curve
B
defined over a finite field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Fix a prime number
\ell
different from the characteristic.
For every place
\nu
of
\mathbb{F}_{q}(B)
, let
z_{\nu}\in\text{CH}_{0}(X_{\nu})
.
Suppose that for all element
A\in\text{Br}(X)\{\ell\}
, we have
\sum_{\nu}\text{Inv}(A(z_{\nu}))=0
. Then for all
n>0
, there is a cycle
z_{n}\in\text{CH}_{0}(X)
such that for all places
\nu
we have that

cl(z_{n})=cl(z_{\nu})\in H^{2d}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X_{\nu},\mu_{\ell^{n}}^{\otimes d}).

Here
\text{Inv}(A(z_{\nu}))
means the value of
(A,z_{\nu})
under the pairing

\text{Br}(X_{\nu})\{\ell\}\times\text{CH}_{0}(X_{\nu})\to\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z},

and
cl:\text{CH}_{0}(X_{v})/\ell^{n}\to H^{2d}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X_{v},\mu_{\ell^{n}}^{\otimes d})
is the cycle class map.

A particular case of the above conjecture is the following.

Conjecture 1.2
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective geometrically integral variety defined over the function field
\mathbb{F}_{q}(B)
of a smooth curve
B
defined over a finite field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Fix a prime number
\ell
different from the characteristic.
Suppose that for every place
\nu
of
\mathbb{F}_{q}(B)
, there is a zero-cycle
z_{\nu}\in\text{CH}_{0}(X_{\nu})
of degree prime to
\ell
.
Suppose that for all elements
A\in\text{Br}(X)\{\ell\}
, we have

\sum_{\nu}\text{Inv}(A(z_{\nu}))=0
.
Then there is a cycle
z\in\text{CH}_{0}(X)
of degree prime to
\ell
.

Conjectures
1.1
and
1.2
are consequences of the following Conjecture
1.3
via considering the commutative diagram of various cycle class maps to the étale cohomology.

Conjecture 1.3

(
[
CT23
, Conjecture 4.2]
)
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective geometrically integral variety defined over a global field
K
. Let
l
be a prime number different from the characteristic of
K
.
When
K
is a number field and
\nu
is a real place, one defines
\text{CH}_{0}(X_{\nu})^{*}=\text{CH}_{0}(X_{\mathbb{R}})/\text{CH}_{0}(X_{\mathbb{C}})
, that is the Chow group over
\mathbb{R}
modulo the image of Chow groups over
\mathbb{C}
by the norm map. For other places,
\text{CH}_{0}(X_{\nu})^{*}
is defined to be the usual Chow group.
Then the Brauer-Manin obstruction is the only obstruction for the local-global principle for zero-cycles on
X
to hold. Namely, there is an exact sequence:

\text{CH}_{0}(X)\hat{\otimes}\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}\to\Pi_{\nu\in\Omega(K)}\text{CH}_{0}(X_{\nu})^{*}\hat{\otimes}\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}\to\text{Hom}(\text{Br}(X)\{\ell\},\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}).

If the cycle class map
\text{CH}_{0}(X_{\nu})\hat{\otimes}\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}\to H^{2d}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X_{\nu},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(d))
is injective for every place
\nu
, Conjecture
1.1
and this stronger conjecture
1.3
are equivalent.
For a discussion of the injectivity for separably rationally connected varieties, see Section
8
.

Unlike the case of rational points, these conjectures apply to all smooth projective varieties.

One of the main theorems of this article (to be proved in Section
6.3
) is the following.

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective geometrically rational surface defined over the function field
\mathbb{F}_{q}(B)
of a smooth projective curve
B
defined over a finite field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Then Conjectures
1.3
, and hence
1.1
and
1.2
hold true for
X
.

By a happy coincidence, we deduce a corollary for rational points (to be proved in Section
6.3
).

Theorem 1.5
.

Let
X
be a del Pezzo surface of degree
4
defined over a global function field of odd characteristic. Then the Brauer-Manin obstruction is the only obstruction for the Hasse principle for rational points on
X
to hold.

1.2.
The Integral Tate conjecture for one-cycles

There is a closely related conjecture.

Conjecture 1.6

(Integral Tate conjecture for one-cycles)
.

Let
V
be a smooth projective geometrically irreducible variety of dimension
d
defined over a finite field
\mathbb{F}
. Fix a prime number
\ell
different from the characteristic. The cycle class map:

(1)

\text{CH}_{1}(V)\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}\to H^{2d-2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(V,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(d-1))

is surjective.

Such a conjecture has been raised many times. See for example
[
CTS10
,
CTK13
,
CTS23
]
.

Saito
[
Sai89
]
and Colliot-Thélène
[
CT99
]
proved that there is a close relation between the surjectivity of this cycle class map and Colliot-Thélène’s conjectures. Namely, Conjecture
1.1
for
X/\mathbb{F}_{q}(B)
follows from the surjectivity of (
1
) for a smooth projective model of
X
. See Section
6
Theorem
6.2
for a more precise statement.

We prove Theorem
1.4
as a consequence of the following theorem, whose proof is contained in Section
6.3
.

Theorem 1.7
.

Let
\pi:\mathcal{X}\to B
be a projective flat family of surfaces over a smooth projective curve
B
defined over a finite field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Fix a prime number
\ell
different from the characteristic. Assume that
\mathcal{X}
is smooth and that the geometric generic fiber is a smooth rational surface. Then the integral Tate conjecture for one-cycles holds. More concretely, the cycle class map

\text{CH}_{1}(\mathcal{X})\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}\to H^{4}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(2))

is surjective.

This theorem is a consequence of our study of one-cycles on a particular class of varieties as defined below.

Definition 1.8
.

Let
X
be a variety defined over a field
k
. Let
K/k
be a field extension, and let
f:\mathbb{P}^{1}_{K}\to X_{K}
be a
K
-morphism whose image lies in the smooth locus of
X_{K}
. The pull-back
f^{*}T_{X_{K}}
decomposes as direct sum of line bundles
\oplus_{i=1}^{\dim X}\mathcal{O}(a_{i})
.
Such a morphism
f
is called a
very free curve
(resp.
free curve
) if
a_{i}>0
(resp.
a_{i}\geq 0
) for all
i
.

We say that
X
is
separably rationally connected
, or
SRC
, if
X
has a very free curve.

We say that
X
is
separably rationally connected in codimension one
or
SRC in codimension one
if
X
has a free curve with
a_{i}>0
for all but one
a_{i}
.

Remark 1.9
.

The term SRC was introduced by Kollár-Miyaoka-Mori
[
KMM92
]
. The term SRC in codimension one was introduced in
[
KT23
]
.

Recall the following definition of algebraic equivalence over a field
k
(not necessarily algebraically closed) as in
[
Ful98
, Definition 10.3]
.

Definition 1.10
.

Let
X
be a
k
-scheme. A
r
-cycle
Z
(
r\geq 0
) is
algebraically equivalent to
0
if there is a non-singular
k
-variety
T
, two
k
-rational points
t_{1},t_{2}\in T
, and a cycle
\mathcal{Z}\in\text{CH}_{\dim T+r}(X\times T)
such that
Z=\mathcal{Z}_{t_{1}}-\mathcal{Z}_{t_{2}}
in
\text{CH}_{r}(X)
, where
\mathcal{Z}_{t_{1}}
(resp.
\mathcal{Z}_{t_{2}}
) is the Gysin pull-back of
\mathcal{Z}
via the inclusion
t_{1}\to T
(resp.
t_{2}\to T
).

Algebraic equivalence is the equivalence relation generated by cycles that are algebraically equivalent to
0
.

We denote by
A_{r}(X)
the group of
r
-cycles in
X
modulo algebraic equivalence, and
\text{CH}_{r}(X)_{\text{alg}}
the subgroup of
\text{CH}_{r}(X)
generated by
r
-cycles algebraically equivalent to
0
.

In general, one can deduce the following geometric criterion for the validity of the integral Tate conjecture, whose proof is contained in Section
6.3
.

Theorem 1.11
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective geometrically integral variety defined over a finite field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
.
Fix a prime number
\ell
different from the characteristic.
Assume that
X
has dimension
d(d\geq 2)
, and is separably rationally connected in codimension one. Consider the following hypotheses:

(A)

The cycle class map
A_{1}(\overline{X})\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}\to H^{2d-2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\overline{X},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(d-1))
is surjective.

(B)

The cycle class map
A_{1}(\overline{X})\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}\to H^{2d-2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\overline{X},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(d-1))
is injective.

(C)

The cycle class map from higher Chow groups (Definition
2.10
)

\lim\limits_{\xleftarrow[n]{}}\text{CH}_{1}(\overline{X},1,\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{n}\mathbb{Z})\to H^{2d-3}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\overline{X},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(d-1))

is surjective.

(D)

The subgroup
N^{d-2}H^{2d-3}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\overline{X},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})
(Definition
1.16
) equals the whole cohomology group
H^{2d-3}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\overline{X},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})
.

The following holds.

(1)

If
\overline{X}
satisfies hypotheses (A) and (B),
then the cycle class map

\text{CH}_{1}(X)\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{l}\to H^{2d-2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{l}(d-1))\to H^{2d-2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\overline{X},\mathbb{Z}_{l}(d-1))^{Gal(\bar{\mathbb{F}}_{q}/\mathbb{F}_{q})}

is surjective.

(2)

If
\overline{X}
satisfies hypothesis (C) or (D),
then the cycle class map

\text{CH}_{1}(X)_{\text{alg}}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{l}\to H^{1}(\mathbb{F}_{q},H^{2d-3}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\overline{X},\mathbb{Z}_{l}(d-1)))

is surjective.

Remark 1.12
.

In dimension
3
, the hypotheses (B), (C), and (D) in Theorem
1.11
are satisfied for a variety that is separably rationally connected in codimension one. See Lemma
5.25
and the proof of Theorem
1.7
in Section
6.3
for details.

Therefore, if a threefold
X
defined over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
that is separably rationally connected in codimension one satisfies hypothesis (A) in the theorem, i.e. the cycle class map
\text{CH}_{1}(\overline{X})\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}\to H^{4}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\overline{X},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(2))
is surjective, then the cycle class map

\text{CH}_{1}({X})\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}\to H^{4}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({X},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(2))

is surjective.

Remark 1.13
.

Hypotheses (A)-(D) only depend on the stable birational class of the generic fiber over
\bar{\mathbb{F}}_{q}(B)
(Lemma
2.36
).
See Section
8
for further discussions about the validity of these hypotheses.

We have a corollary of Theorem
1.7
, which confirms a conjecture of Colliot-Thélène and Kahn (
[
CTK13
, Conjecture 5.8]
) up to
p
-torsion. The proof is contained in Section
6.3
.

Corollary 1.14
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective threefold defined over a finite field
\mathbb{F}
.
Fix a prime number
\ell
different from the characteristic.
Assume that
X
admits a fibration structure over a smooth projective curve with smooth projective geometrically rational generic fiber.
Then we have

H^{3}_{\text{nr}}(X,\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}/\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(2))=0,

\text{CH}_{1}(X)\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{4}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(2)),

and a short exact sequence

0\to H^{1}(\mathbb{F},H^{3}(\overline{X},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(2))\{\ell\})\to\text{CH}_{1}(X)\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}\to\text{CH}_{1}(\overline{X})^{G}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}\to 0.

1.3.
Method of proof

We use a geometric approach based on the technique developed in a joint work of the author with János Kollár
[
KT23
]
.
This technique, very roughly speaking, allows one to turn algebraic equivalence into deformations of stable maps, which is the key geometric input behind all the results proved in this paper.

We sketch the proof of Theorems
1.7
and
1.11
, which are contained in Section
6.3
.

The Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence gives an exact sequence:

0\to H^{1}(\mathbb{F}_{q},H^{2d-2r-1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\overline{X},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(d-r)))\to H^{2d-2r}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(d-r))\to H^{2d-2r}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\overline{X},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(d-r))^{G}\to 0.

Thus the integral Tate conjecture consists of a geometric part, i.e. surjectivity of

\text{CH}_{r}(X)\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}\to H^{2d-2r}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\overline{X},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(d-r))^{G},

and an arithmetic part, i.e. surjectivity of

\text{CH}_{r}(X)_{\text{hom}}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}\to H^{1}(\mathbb{F}_{q},H^{2d-2r-1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\overline{X},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(d-r))),

where
\text{CH}_{r}(X)_{\text{hom}}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}
is the “geometrically homologically trivial” part, i.e. the kernel of

\text{CH}_{r}(X)\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}\to H^{2d-2r}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\overline{X},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(d-r))^{G}.

For the geometric part, we need the following theorem, obtained in the joint work with J. Kollár
[
KT23
]
.

Theorem 1.15
.

[
KT23
, Theorem 7]

Let
X_{k}
be a smooth projective variety defined over a perfect field
k
. Assume that every geometrically irreducible
k
-variety has a
0
-cycle of degree
1
(e.g.
k
is a finite field or a PAC field). Assume that
X
is separably rationally connected in codimension one.
We have an isomorphism

A_{1}(X_{k})\xrightarrow{\cong}A_{1}(X_{\bar{k}})^{G},

where
G
is the absolute Galois group of
k
.

We apply this theorem to prove the geometric part (
1
) of Theorem
1.11
.

For the arithmetic part, we recall the following definitions, recently revisited by Benoist-Ottem, Voisin, Scavia-Suzuki, and Benoist in
[
BO21
,
Voi22
,
SS22
,
Ben22
]
.

Definition 1.16
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety of dimension
d
defined over an algebraically closed field.
Given an abelian group
A
that is one of
\mathbb{Z}/m\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell},\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Q}
, or
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
, where
m
is a positive integer,
\ell
a prime number, both of which are relative prime to the field characteristic, we simply write
H^{k}(X,A)
as either the étale cohomology with coefficient
A
or the singular cohomology with coefficient
A
(if
X
is a complex variety), and
H_{i}(*,A)
as the étale Borel-Moore homology or singular Borel-Moore homology. We define the following filtrations on the cohomology group
H^{k}(X,A)
.

(1)

The coniveau filtration
\{N^{*}H^{k}(X,A)\}
with

N^{c}H^{k}(X,A):=\sum_{f:Y\to X}f_{*}(H_{2d-k}(Y,A))\subset H_{2d-k}(X,A)\cong H^{k}(X,A),

where the sum is taken over all morphisms from projective algebraic sets
f:Y\to X,\dim Y\leq d-c
;

(2)

The strong coniveau filtration
\{\tilde{N}^{*}H^{k}(X,A)\}
with

\tilde{N}^{c}H^{k}(X,A):=\sum_{f:Y\to X}f_{*}(H_{2d-k}(Y,A))\subset H_{2d-k}(X,A)\cong H^{k}(X,A),

where the sum is taken over all morphisms from
smooth
projective varieties
f:Y\to X,\dim Y\leq d-c
.

Remark 1.17
.

If
X
is smooth but not necessarily projective, we may similarly define the coniveau filtration by taking the sum over all algebraic sets
Y
with a proper morphism
f:Y\to X
.

In a recent preprint
[
SS23
]
, Scavia and Suzuki systematically investigated the question of the surjectivity in the arithmetic part and related this question to the comparison of the strong coniveau filtration and the coniveau filtration.

Proposition 1.18
.

[
SS23
, Proposition 5.6]

Let
X
be a smooth projective geometrically connected variety over a finite field
\mathbb{F}
and
\ell
be a prime number invertible in
\mathbb{F}
. Then the algebraic Walker Abel-Jacobi map restricted to cycles algebraically equivalent to
0

\text{CH}^{r}(X)_{\text{alg}}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}\to H^{1}(\mathbb{F},N^{r-1}H^{2r-1}(\overline{X},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(r)))

has the same image as

H^{1}(\mathbb{F},\tilde{N}^{r-1}H^{2r-1}(\overline{X},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(r)))\to H^{1}(\mathbb{F},{N}^{r-1}H^{2r-1}(\overline{X},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(r))).

Here the algebraic Walker Abel-Jacobi map (restricted to cycles algebraically equivalent to
0
)

\text{CH}^{r}(X)_{\text{alg}}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}\to H^{1}(\mathbb{F},N^{r-1}H^{2r-1}(\overline{X},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(r)))

is a lifting of the cycle class map

\text{CH}^{r}(X)_{\text{alg}}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}\to H^{1}(\mathbb{F},H^{2r-1}(\overline{X},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(r))),

constructed in
[
SS23
, Formula (4.2), Section 4.2]
.

Remark 1.19
.

In the paper of Scavia-Suzuki
[
SS23
]
, the authors use
\text{CH}^{r}(X)_{\text{F-alg}}
to denote the group
\text{CH}^{r}(X)_{\text{alg}}
in our notations, and use
\text{CH}^{r}(X)_{\text{alg}}
to denote a different group that contains
\text{CH}^{r}(X)_{\text{F-alg}}
(see the first two paragraphs in
[
SS23
, Section 4.1]
).

Therefore part (
2
) of Theorem
1.11
is implied by the following theorem, which generalizes a result of Voisin in
[
Voi22
]
(see Section
2.4
for a detailed account).

Theorem 1.20

(=Theorem
5.16
)
.

Let
X
be a
d
-dimensional smooth projective variety defined over an algebraically closed field, which is separably rationally connected in codimension one. Fix a prime number
\ell
distinct from the characteristic.
Then the following two subgroups of
H^{2d-3}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})
agree:

\tilde{N}^{d-2}H_{\text{\'{e}t}}^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})=N^{d-2}H^{2d-3}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}).

The proof relies on the techniques developed in the joint work with Kollár
[
KT23
]
, which allows us to prove a structural result about the space of one-cycles on a smooth projective variety that is SRC in codimension one in Section
3
Theorem
3.11
, and deduce its consequences for the various coniveau filtrations in Section
4
and Section
5
.

Finally, combining results of Esnault-Wittenberg
[
EW16
]
, and Bloch-Srinivas
[
BS83
]
(which uses Merkujev-Suslin’s theorem on
K_{2}

[
MS82
, Theorem 11.5]
), one proves that hypotheses (A), (B), (C) and (D) in Theorem
1.11
holds for rational surface fibrations over a curve. This proves Theorem
1.7
.

1.4.
Related work

1.4.1.
The Integral Tate conjecture for one-cycles

Colliot-Thélène and Kahn studied the surjectivity of the
\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}
-coefficient cycle class map of codimension
2
cycles and its relation with the degree
3
unramified cohomology
H^{3}_{\text{nr}}(X,\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}/\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(2))
in
[
CTK13
]
(over the complex numbers, such a relation is studied in
[
CTV12
]
). For more information, see Section
6
.

Over finite fields, several groups of authors proved the vanishing of the degree
3
unramified cohomology on certain threefolds and deduced the integral Tate conjecture for one-cycles, and thus proving Conjecture
1.1
and
1.2
for some surfaces defined over a global function field. See, for example,
[
PS16
]
for the case of conic bundles over a surface for which the Tate conjecture is known,
[
CTS23
]
and
[
Sca22
]
for the case of a product of a curve with a
\text{CH}_{0}
-trivial surface.

1.4.2.
The Local-global principle

There is a vast literature on the local-global principles for zero-cycles/rational points on geometrically rational surfaces. Let us only mention a few relevant results and refer the readers to survey articles such as
[
Wit18
]
etc. for a more comprehensive list.

Work of Colliot-Thélène
[
CT00
]
made important progress towards Conjecture
1.3
for ruled surfaces defined over number fields. Together with later works of Frossard
[
Fro03
]
, van Hamel
[
vH03
]
, they proved that Conjecture
1.3
holds if the Jacobian of the base curve has finite Tate-Shafarevich group.
The global function field version for ruled surfaces is studied by Parimala-Suresh
[
PS16
]
, which depends on the computation of the degree
3
unramified cohomology as explained above.
Assuming that the base surface satisfies the Tate conjecture, they establishes the integral Tate conjecture for conic bundle over surfaces defined over finite fields and Conjecture
1.3
.
Colliot-Thélène-Swinnerton-Dyer
[
CTSD12
]
studied the integral Tate conjecture and the local-global principle for zero-cycles on surfaces of the form
(f+tg=0)\subset\mathbb{P}^{3}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{t}
. In addition to proving that the integral Tate conjecture for
1
-cycles holds for such surfaces, for cubic surfaces of this form, they also prove that the existence of rational points is equivalent to the existence of a zero-cycle of degree
1
for such surfaces.

The study of complete intersections of two quadrics has also drawn a lot of attention. It started with the work of Colliot-Thélène, Sansuc, and Swinnerton-Dyer
[
CTSSD87
]
. Heath-Brown proved that the Hasse principle for rational points holds for smooth complete intersections of two quadrics in
\mathbb{P}^{7}
over number fields
[
HB18
]
, see also a different proof by Colliot-Thélène
[
CT24
]
. Under the assumption on the finiteness of the Tate-Shafarevich group of elliptic curves and the validity of Schinzel’s hypothesis, Wittenberg proved that the Hasse principle holds for such complete intersections in
\mathbb{P}^{5}
and some cases in
\mathbb{P}^{4}
(with the Brauer-Manin obstruction) over number fields
[
Wit07
]
.
The author has shown in a previous paper
[
Tia17
]
that the Hasse principle for rational points holds for smooth complete intersections of two quadrics in
\mathbb{P}^{n}(n\geq 5)
defined over a global function field of odd characteristic.

One is also interested in weak approximation for rational points on a del Pezzo surface of degree
4
.
For a del Pezzo surface of degree
4
over a number field, assuming that there is a rational point, Salberger and Skorobogatov
[
SS91
]
proved that the Brauer-Manin obstruction is the only obstruction to weak approximation.
As the author has been informed by Colliot-Thélène, essentially the same argument also proves that over a global function field of odd characteristic, the Brauer-Manin obstruction is the only obstruction to weak approximation once there is a rational point.
In characteristic
2
, some partial results are contained in the joint work of the author with Letao Zhang
[
TZ18
]
.

1.4.3.
Coniveau and strong coniveau filtration

Voisin
[
Voi22
]
was the first to study the two filtrations on
H^{2d-3}
for rationally connected varieties and threefolds over the complex numbers.
In particular, she related the strong coniveau filtration to the filtration given by families of one-cycles (Lemma
2.33
), a strategy that we also follow.
On the other hand, there are many examples showing that the two filtrations differ for a general smooth projective variety
[
BO21
,
SS22
,
Ben22
]
.

1.5.
Structure of the paper

Section
2
consists of some preliminary results.
We explain some basic properties of being SRC in codimension one (Section
2.1
), the Bloch-Kato conjecture (the norm residue isomorphism theorem), the Beilinson-Lichtenbaum conjecture (Section
2.2
), and functoriality of the higher cycle class map from Bloch’s higher Chow groups to Borel-Moore homology (Section
2.3
), several notions of filtrations due to Voisin/Benoist-Ottem (Section
2.4
), and we verify that the hypotheses in Theorem
1.11
only depends on the stable birational class of the generic fiber over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}(B)
(Section
2.5
).

The main parts of the paper can be seen as applications of the general method developed in
[
KT23
]
.
The first application is in Section
3
, where we describe some structural results of the space of one-cycles. We use Corollary 35 and Theorem 48 in
[
KT23
]
to prove Theorems
3.10
,
3.11
about the space of one-cycles in Section
3
.
These structural results are then used in Sections
4

5
to study the coniveau filtration on
H_{3}
of a smooth projective variety that is separably rationally connected in codimension one (Theorems
4.16
,
5.16
), which, in turn, is the foundation for the main theorems about the integral Tate conjecture (Theorems
1.11
,
1.7
) and the local-global principles (Theorem
1.4
), all of which are proved in Section
6.3
.

In Section
4
, we study the coniveau filtration on
H_{3}
of complex varieties using Lawson homology and the result is topological in nature.
These results are not needed for the arithmetic applications.
But the proof illustrates the main ideas without using the heavy machinery of Chow sheaves.
Moreover, this approach gives “integral” results, some of which are stronger than the results that one can obtain in the general case.
We present this first to give the readers some flavor of the argument.
To deal with the general case requires the use of Chow sheaves introduced by Suslin and Voevodsky in
[
SV00b
]
, hence more abstract.
Unfortunately, in this case we only have results for torsion coefficients and have to pass to the inverse limit from time to time.
The criterion for the surjectivity in the arithmetic part is proved in Corollary
5.27
.

Section
6
reviews the relation between the integral Tate conjecture for one-cycles, the degree
3
unramified cohomology, and the local-global principle. The main theorems are proved in Section
6.3
.

In Section
7
, we give some examples where the criterion in Theorem
1.11
can be effectively checked.
Section
8
consists of some questions and expectations.

The following diagram shows the dependence of the main theorems.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section we collect some basic results that will be useful later, most (if not all) of which are well-known to experts.
We include the proof for those results for which the author does not know a proof in the published literature for the sake of completeness.

2.1.
Separable rational connectedness in codimension one

We prove some basic properties of varieties that are SRC in codimension one in this section.

Definition 2.1
.

A
separably rationally connected fibration over a curve
is a flat morphism from a normal proper variety
Y
to a smooth proper curve
C
such that the smooth locus of the geometric generic fiber has a very free curve.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
X
be a smooth proper variety that is separably rationally connected in codimension one. Then
X
is either birational to a separably rationally connected fibration over a curve, or separably rationally connected, or rationally connected by a family of free curves. The third case only happens in positive characteristic.

Proof.

One can take the rational quotient of the variety
X
by the relation generated by free rational curves (
[
Kol96
, Example IV.4.11, Theorem IV4.13]
or
[
Sta06
, Theorem 1.3]
).
This gives an open subset
U\subset X
and a morphism
U\to Z
to a variety
Z
such that the closure of a fiber over a geometric point of
Z
is the equivalence class of points connected by a chain of free rational curves.
The SRC in codimension one condition implies that for any general geometric point, there is at least one free rational curve passing through it such that the deformation of this free rational curve containing the geometric point covers at least a divisor in
X
. So the quotient
Z
is either a curve or a point.

If
Z
is a point, then two general points of
X
are connected by a chain of free rational curves. A general smoothing of this chain of free rational curves gives a free rational curve that connects two general points of
X
. That is,
X
is rationally connected by a family of free rational curves in the terminology of
[
She10
]
. In characteristic
0
, separable rational connectedness is the same as rational connectedness for smooth proper varieties.

If
Z
is a curve, we may assume that
Z
is smooth projective and extend the morphism to an open subset
V\subset X
so that the complement of
V
has codimension at least
2
. General deformations of free rational curves lie in
V
. Lemma 2.5 in
[
Sta06
]
says that every free rational curve in
V
that has non-empty intersection with the smooth locus of
V\to Z
lie in the smooth locus. In particular, by definition of SRC in codimension one, there are free rational curves in the smooth locus of
V\to Z
which are very free when considered as curves in the fiber. So
X
is birational to a separably rationally connected fibration over a curve that is a normal projective compactification of
V
with a fibration structure over
Z
.
∎

Remark 2.3
.

In positive characteristic, examples of the third kind can be constructed using purely inseparable covers of SRC varieties (
[
Kol96
, Example V.5.19]
). They belong to the class of
freely rationally connected varieties
studied in
[
She10
]
.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
X
be a variety defined over an algebraically closed field
k
and
Z\subset X
a closed subset, endowed with the reduced closed subscheme structure. The rational Chow group of zero-cycles on
X
is
universally supported in
Z
if for every algebraically closed field extension
L/k
, the map

\text{CH}_{0}(Z_{L})\otimes\mathbb{Q}\to\text{CH}_{0}(X_{L})\otimes\mathbb{Q}

is surjective.

The following is well-known (see for example
[
BS83
, Proposition 1]
).

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
X
be a smooth proper variety defined over an algebraically closed field
k
and
Z\subset X
a closed subset. The rational Chow group of zero-cycles on
X
is universally supported in
Z
if and only if there is a decomposition of the diagonal of the form

N\Delta_{X}=\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2}\in\text{CH}_{\dim X}(X\times X),

where
N
is an integer,
\Gamma_{1}
is a cycle supported in
X\times Z
, and
\Gamma_{2}
is a cycle supported in
D\times X
for some divisor
D\subset X
.

As a corollary of the structure of varieties that are SRC in codimension one, we have the following.

Corollary 2.6
.

Let
X
be a smooth proper variety that is separably rationally connected in codimension one. Then the rational Chow group of zero-cycles on
X
is universally supported on a curve.
Equivalently, there is a one dimensional closed subset
Z\subset X
, a divisor
D\subset X
, and a decomposition of the diagonal of the form

N\Delta_{X}=\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2}\in\text{CH}_{\dim X}(X\times X),

where
N
is an integer,
\Gamma_{1}
is a cycle supported in
X\times Z
, and
\Gamma_{2}
is a cycle supported in
D\times X
.

Proof.

Because of the structure of
X
described in Lemma
2.2
, we may take the curve to be a general smooth complete intersection of very ample divisors. Then every geometric point is connected to such a curve by a chain of rational curves. The rest follows from Lemma
2.5
.
∎

2.2.
The Bloch-Kato conjecture and the Beilinson-Lichtenbaum conjecture

In this section, we collect several results related to the Bloch-Kato conjecture and give references.

Theorem 2.7

(the Bloch-Kato conjecture, or the norm residue isomorphism theorem,
[
Voe11
, Theorem 6.1]
)
.

Let
k
be a field and
m
a positive integer relatively prime to the characteristic of
k
. The norm residue map from Milnor K-group to étale cohomology induces an isomorphism

K^{M}_{i}(k)/mK^{M}_{i}(k)\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k,\mu_{m}^{\otimes i})

for all non-negative integers
i
.

The case
i=2
, also called the Merkurjev-Suslin theorem on
K_{2}
, is proved by Merkurjev-Suslin earlier
[
MS82
, Theorem 11.5]
.
It is known that the Bloch-Kato conjecture is equivalent to the following conjecture of Beilinson-Lichtenbaum (
[
SV00a
, Theorem 7.4]
,
[
GL01
, Theorem 1.1]
).

Theorem 2.8

(the Beilinson-Lichtenbaum conjecture)
.

Fix a field
k
. For all positive integers
q
, for all positive integers
m
relatively prime to the characteristic of
k
, the natural map

\mathbb{Z}(q)\otimes\mathbb{Z}/m\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{\cong}\tau^{\leq q}R\pi_{*}(\mu_{m}^{\otimes q})

is an isomorphism in the derived category of Zariski sheaves on a smooth
variety
X
over
k
, where
\mathbb{Z}(q)(q\geq 0)
is the motivic complex defined in
[
SV00a
, Definition 3.1]
,
\pi:X_{\text{\'{e}t}}\to X_{\text{Zar}}
is the morphism from the étale site of
X
to the Zariski site of
X
, and
\tau
is the good truncation functor.

For later use, we record the following corollary. It is well-known to experts, but it is not so easy to find a proof in the literature. Over the complex numbers with singular cohomology, the statement is
[
BO21
, Proposition 2.8]
, which depends on
[
CTV12
, Théorème 3.1]
.

Corollary 2.9
.

Let
X
be a smooth variety defined over an algebraically closed field
k
. Fix
\ell
a prime number distinct from the characteristic of
k
. All the torsion classes in the étale cohomology
H^{i+1}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(i))(i\geq 0)
are supported in divisors, and
H^{i+1}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(i))/N^{1}H^{i+1}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(i))
is torsion free.

The same conclusion holds if
X
is a complex variety and we use singular cohomology with
\mathbb{Z}
-coefficients.

Proof.

For each positive integer
n
, we have a long exact sequence

\ldots\to H^{i}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(i))\xrightarrow{\cdot\ell^{n}}H^{i}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(i))\xrightarrow{\mod\ell^{n}}H^{i}(X,\mu_{\ell^{n}}^{\otimes i})\to H^{i+1}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(i))\to\ldots,

and a similar long exact sequence for any open subset of
X
.
A torsion class
\alpha_{0}
in
H^{i+1}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(i))
comes from a class
\alpha\in H^{i}(X,\mu_{\ell^{n}}^{\otimes i})
for some
n
.
By the Bloch-Kato conjecture (Theorem
2.7
), we have a surjection

K_{i}^{M}(k(X))\to K_{i}^{M}(k(X))/\ell^{n}\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{i}(k(X),\mu_{\ell^{n}}^{\otimes i}).

So the image of
\alpha
in
H^{i}(k(X),\mu_{\ell^{n}}^{\otimes i})

is the image of a finite sum of symbols

\sum_{j}(a_{j1},\ldots,a_{ji}),a_{j1},\ldots,a_{ji}\in k(X)^{*}.

There is an open subset
U\subset X
, where all the rational functions involved in this finite sum of symbols are regular functions on
U
, and thus define a class
\tilde{\alpha}\in K_{i}^{M}(U)
.
Then
\tilde{\alpha}
defines a cohomology class
[\tilde{\alpha}]
in

H^{i}(U,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(i))=\lim\limits_{\xleftarrow[m]{}}H^{i}(U,\mu_{\ell^{m}}^{\otimes i})

via the compatible maps

\wedge^{i}H^{0}(U,\mathbb{G}_{m})\to\wedge^{i}H^{1}(U,\mu_{\ell^{m}})\to H^{i}(U,\mu_{\ell^{m}}^{\otimes i}).

The image of the cohomology class
[\tilde{\alpha}]
under the composition

H^{i}(U,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(i))\xrightarrow{\mod\ell^{n}}H^{i}(U,\mu_{\ell^{m}}^{\otimes i})\to H^{i}(k(X),\mu_{\ell^{n}}^{\otimes i})

maps to the image of
\alpha
in
H^{i}(k(X),\mu_{\ell^{n}}^{\otimes i})
.
So there is an open subset
V\subset U
such that the cohomology class
[\tilde{\alpha}]|_{V}

maps to
\alpha|_{V}
via the map

H^{i}(V,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(i))\xrightarrow{\mod\ell^{n}}H^{i}(V,\mu_{\ell^{n}}^{\otimes i}).

Therefore
\alpha_{0}|_{V}=0
.
That is, the class
\alpha_{0}
is supported in a divisor.

For complex varieties,
[
BO21
, Proposition 2.8]
, which depends on
[
CTV12
, Théorème 3.1]
, shows that torsion classes in singular cohomology with
\mathbb{Z}
-coefficient are supported on divisors.

Let
\alpha
be a class in
H^{i+1}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(i))
that becomes torsion in

H^{i+1}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(i))/N^{1}H^{i+1}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(i)).

Then some multiple of
\alpha
is supported in a codimension one algebraic set
D
in
X
. We have the localization sequence for étale cohomology:

\ldots\to H^{i+1}_{D}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(i))\to H^{i+1}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(i))\to H^{i+1}(X\backslash D,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(i))\to\ldots.

Since torsion classes are supported on divisors for
any
smooth varieties, in particular,
X\backslash D
, there is a codimension one algebraic set
D^{\prime}
in
X
such that
\alpha
restrict to the zero class in
H^{i+1}(X\backslash(D\cup D^{\prime}),\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(i))
. That is,
\alpha
is supported in
D\cup D^{\prime}
.

The same argument works for singular cohomology on smooth complex varieties.
∎

2.3.
Higher Chow groups, Borel-Moore homology, and higher cycle class map

Higher Chow groups were first defined by Bloch in
[
Blo86
]
.

Definition 2.10
.

Let
k
be a scheme and let
X
be an equi-dimensional finite type
k
-scheme. We write
z^{q}(X,p)
for the free abelian group generated by all codimension
q
subvarieties on
X\times\Delta^{p}
which intersect all faces
X\times\Delta^{j}
properly for all
j<p
, where

\Delta^{p}=\text{Spec }k[t_{0},\ldots,t_{p}]/\langle\sum_{j}t_{j}=1\rangle

is the algebraic
p
-simplex.
We write
z^{q}(X,*)
for the associated chain complex, whose boundary maps are alternating sums of restrictions to faces.

For any abelian group
A
, the higher Chow group with coefficient
A
, denoted by
\text{CH}^{q}(X,p,A)
, is the
p
-th homology of the complex
z^{q}(X,*)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}A
.
We define
\text{CH}_{i}(X,j,A)
as
\text{CH}^{\dim X-i}(X,j,A)
. We may omit
A
if
A=\mathbb{Z}
.

Later, higher Chow groups were shown to be naturally isomorphic to motivic cohomology for smooth varieties.

Theorem 2.11
.

[
Voe02
, Corollary 2]

For any field
k
, any smooth variety
X
over
k
and any
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, there is a natural isomorphism

H^{2q-p}(X,\mathbb{Z}(q))\xrightarrow{\cong}\text{CH}^{q}(X,p),

and the same holds for the motivic cohomology and higher Chow groups with coefficients.

By definition, the groups in the above theorem vanish if
p<0
or
q<0
.
The motivic cohomology group
H^{2q-p}(X,\mathbb{Z}(q))
vanishes if
2q-p<0
and
X
is smooth
[
MVW06
, Theorem 19.3]
.

As a corollary of this theorem and the Beilinson-Lichtenbaum conjecture
2.8
,
we have

Corollary 2.12
.

Let
X
be a smooth variety over a perfect field
k
and
m
an integer relatively prime to the characteristic. For
p\geq 0,q\geq 0
, we have an isomorphism

\text{CH}^{q}(X,p,\mathbb{Z}/m)\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{2q-p}(X,\mathbb{Z}(q)\otimes\mathbb{Z}/m)\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{\text{Zar}}^{2q-p}(X,\tau^{\leq q}R\pi_{*}(\mu_{m}^{\otimes q})).

At least for the purpose of this paper, it is more natural to view higher Chow groups as a Borel-Moore type homology theory.
And one can define a higher cycle class map to the Borel-Moore homology over a field
k
. This material is well-known to experts. When
k
is an algebraically closed field, we find the details in
[
KZ23
, Section 4]
.

Definition 2.13
.

For a scheme
f:X\to\text{Spec }k
defined over a perfect field
k
, and fix
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}/m\mathbb{Z}
such that
m
is relatively prime to the characteristic of
k
, the Borel-Moore homology is defined as

H^{\text{BM}}_{n}(X,\Lambda(i)):=H^{-n}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,f^{!}\Lambda(-i)).

For a prime number
\ell
different from the characteristic of
k
, we define

H^{\text{BM}}_{n}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(i)):=\lim\limits_{\xleftarrow[s]{}}H^{\text{BM}}_{n}(X,\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{s}(i)).

In particular, if
X
is smooth of pure dimension
d
,
f^{!}=f^{*}
and we have a natural isomorphism

(2)

H^{\text{BM}}_{n}(X,\Lambda(i))\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{\text{\'{e}t}}^{2d-n}(X,\Lambda(d-i)).

Borel-Moore homology is functorial for proper push-forward, étale pull-back, and satisfies the localization long exact sequence (
[
BO74
, Example 2.1]
). Since
f^{!}=f^{*}
on smooth varieties, for a morphism
g:X\to Y
between smooth varieties, we have pull-back via the isomorphism (
2
):

g^{*}:H^{\text{BM}}_{2\dim Y-n}(Y,\Lambda(\dim Y-i))\to H^{\text{BM}}_{2\dim X-n}(X,\Lambda(\dim X-i)).

In
[
KZ23
, Definition 4.2]
, for any variety
X
defined over an algebraically closed field, the authors define a higher cycle class map

(3)

cl_{i,j}:\text{CH}_{i}(X,j,\Lambda)\to H^{\text{BM}}_{2i+j}(X,\Lambda(i)).

It seems to the author that it is not necessary to assume that the base field is algebraically closed for their definition and results that we quote in the following to hold.
But to be consistent with the reference
[
KZ23
]
, and since we will only need the results for varieties defined over algebraically closed fields, we work over an algebraically closed field
k
in the following.

For smooth varieties over an algebraically closed field
k
, by identifying the Borel-Moore homology with étale cohomology, this cycle class map can also be defined as (
[
KZ23
, Definition 4.2]
)

(4)

cl^{d-i,j}:\text{CH}^{d-i}(X,j,\Lambda)\to H_{\text{\'{e}t}}^{2d-2i-j}(X,\Lambda(d-i)).

Remark 2.14
.

The higher cycle class map (
3
) is defined via a change of topology: higher Chow group of
X
is computed as the Hom-set between two objects in the derived category of Voevodsky motives in the cdh topology
[
KZ23
, Proposition 4.1]
:

\text{CH}_{i}(X,j,\Lambda)\cong\text{Hom}_{DM_{cdh}}(\Lambda(i)[2i+j],Rf_{*}f^{!}\Lambda),

while Borel-Moore homology is computed as the Hom-set between two objects in the derived category of Voevodsky motives in the h topology:

H_{2i+j}^{\text{BM}}(X,\Lambda(i))\cong\text{Hom}_{DM_{h}}(\Lambda(i)[2i+j],Rf_{*}f^{!}\Lambda).

Then the higher cycle class map is induced by the “realization functor” (
[
CD15
, 9.1.1]
)

DM_{cdh}\to DM_{h}.

The definition of higher cycle class maps for smooth varieties is obtained from this higher cycle class map by applying duality in
DM_{cdh}
and
DM_{h}
(
[
KZ23
, Definition 4.2]
).

Proposition 2.15

(
[
KZ23
, Proposition 4.3]
)
.

For varieties defined over an algebraically closed field, the higher cycle class map
cl_{i,j}
defined above is functorial with respect to proper push-forward, étale pull-back, and compatible with the localization long exact sequence.

For smooth varieties, Geisser-Levine defines a higher cycle class map
[
GL01
]
(see formula (3.3) and the ensuing paragraph).

(5)

\displaystyle cl^{q,p}:

\displaystyle\text{CH}^{q}(X,p,\mathbb{Z}/m)\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{\text{Zar}}^{2q-p}(X,\tau^{\leq q}R\pi_{*}(\mu_{m}^{\otimes q}))

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle H_{\text{Zar}}^{2q-p}(X,R\pi_{*}(\mu_{m}^{\otimes q}))\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{2q-p}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,\mu_{m}^{\otimes q}),p,q\geq 0.

Proposition 2.16
.

[
KZ23
, Proposition 4.4]

For smooth varieties defined over an algebraically closed field, the two definitions (
4
), (
5
) agree (up to a natural isomorphism).

As an immediate corollary, we have the following.

Corollary 2.17
.

Let
X
be a reduced finite type scheme defined over an algebraically closed field. Fix a positive integer
m
that is relatively prime to the field characteristic. Then for any
n,0\leq n\leq 2d
, the higher cycle class map induces an isomorphism

cl_{0,n}:\text{CH}_{0}(X,n,\mathbb{Z}/m)\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{n}^{\text{BM}}(X,\mathbb{Z}/m).

Proof.

If
X
is smooth of equi-dimension
d
, the higher cycle class map is a composition of isomorphisms

\displaystyle\text{CH}_{0}(X,n,\mathbb{Z}/m)\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{\text{Zar}}^{2d-n}(X,\tau^{\leq d}R\pi_{*}(\mu_{m}^{\otimes d}))

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}

\displaystyle H_{\text{Zar}}^{2d-n}(X,R\pi_{*}(\mu_{m}^{\otimes d}))\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{2d-n}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,\mu_{m}^{\otimes d})\cong H_{n}^{\text{BM}}(X,\mathbb{Z}/m),

where for the second isomorphism, we use the fact that
R\pi_{*}(\mu_{m}^{\otimes d})
has no cohomology in degree
d+1
and higher.

In general, we use induction on dimension. Dimension
0
case is trivially true. Write
X
as the disjoint union of its singular locus
Z
and smooth locus
U
. Then the statement is true for both
Z
(by induction hypothesis) and
U
(because it is smooth). Since the cycle class maps are compatible with the localization sequence for higher Chow groups and Borel-Moore homology (Proposition
2.15
), we have a commutative diagram of localization long exact sequences for higher Chow groups and Borel-Moore homology with
\mathbb{Z}/m
-coefficients:

\begin{CD}\ldots\text{CH}_{0}(Z,j)@>{}>{}>\text{CH}_{0}(X,j)@>{}>{}>\text{CH}_{0}(U,j)@>{}>{}>\text{CH}_{0}(Z,j-1)\ldots\\
@V{}V{cl_{0,j}^{Z}}V@V{}V{cl_{0,j}^{X}}V@V{}V{cl_{0,j}^{U}}V@V{}V{cl_{0,j-1}^{Z}}V\\
\ldots H_{j}^{\text{BM}}(Z)@>{}>{}>H_{j}^{\text{BM}}(X)@>{}>{}>H_{j}^{\text{BM}}(U)@>{}>{}>H_{j-1}^{\text{BM}}(Z)\ldots.\\
\end{CD}

So the desired isomorphism follows from five lemma.
∎

Another immediate corollary of identifying the two higher cycle class maps
[
KZ23
, Proposition 4.4]
is the following.

Corollary 2.18

(
[
GL01
, Proposition 4.2(i), 4.7]
)
.

The higher cycle class maps (
4
), (
5
) are compatible with pull-back between smooth varieties and the product structure on higher Chow groups and the étale cohomology for smooth varieties defined over an algebraically closed field.

Let
X,Y
be smooth projective varieties and
\Gamma\in\text{CH}_{\dim X+n}(X\times Y)\otimes\Lambda
be a correspondence. Since correspondence actions are compositions of pulling-back, taking product, and pushing-forward, we have the following.

Proposition 2.19
.

The higher cycle class maps for smooth projective varieties (
4
) (and hence also (
5
)) are compatible with correspondence action.

Lemma 2.20
.

Let
X
be a variety defined over a field
k
. Fix a short exact sequence of abelian groups

0\to A\xrightarrow{f}B\xrightarrow{g}C\to 0.

There is a long exact sequence of higher Chow groups with coefficients in
A,B,C
:

\ldots\to\text{CH}^{q}(X,p,A)\xrightarrow{id\otimes f}\text{CH}^{q}(X,p,B)\xrightarrow{id\otimes g}\text{CH}_{q}(X,p,C)\to\text{CH}^{q}(X,p-1,A)\to\ldots,

for
0\leq q\leq\dim X
.

Proof.

This comes from taking homology of the short exact sequence of complexes:

0\to z^{q}(X,*)\otimes A\xrightarrow{id\otimes f}z^{q}(X,*)\otimes B\xrightarrow{id\otimes g}z^{q}(X,*)\otimes C\to 0.

We have injection on the left because the group
z^{q}(X,p)
is free.
∎

Finally, we define the higher cycle class maps

\text{CH}_{i}(X,j,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})\to H_{2i+j}^{\text{BM}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(i))

by the composition

\text{CH}_{i}(X,j,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})\to\lim\limits_{\xleftarrow[n]{}}\text{CH}_{i}(X,j,\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{n})\to\lim\limits_{\xleftarrow[n]{}}H_{2i+j}^{\text{BM}}(X,\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{n}(i))=H_{2i+j}^{\text{BM}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(i)).

With this definition, we have the following.

Proposition 2.21
.

Let
X
be a variety defined over an algebraically closed field and
\ell
a prime number different from the field characteristic. We have a commutative diagram of long exact sequences:

\begin{CD}\ldots\to\text{CH}_{i}(X,j,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})@>{\times\ell^{n}}>{}>\text{CH}_{i}(X,j,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})@>{}>{}>\text{CH}_{i}(X,j,\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{n})\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
\ldots\to H_{2i+j}^{\text{BM}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(i))@>{\times\ell^{n}}>{}>H_{2i+j}^{\text{BM}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(i))@>{}>{}>H_{2i+j}^{\text{BM}}(X,\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{n}(i))\end{CD}

\begin{CD}\to{\text{CH}}_{i}(X,j-1,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})@>{\times\ell^{n}}>{}>\text{CH}_{i}(X,j-1,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})@>{}>{}>\text{CH}_{i}(X,j-1,\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{n})\ldots\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
\to H_{2i+j-1}^{\text{BM}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(i))@>{\times\ell^{n}}>{}>H_{2i+j-1}^{\text{BM}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(i))@>{}>{}>H_{2i+j-1}^{\text{BM}}(X,\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{n}(i))\ldots\end{CD}

Proof.

We have a commutative diagram of short exact sequences of coefficients (
n,m\geq 0
):

\begin{CD}0@>{}>{}>\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}@>{\times\ell^{n}}>{}>\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}@>{\mod\ell^{n}}>{}>\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{n}@>{}>{}>0\\
\Big\|@V{}V{\mod\ell^{m}}V@V{}V{\mod\ell^{n+m}}V\Big\|\Big\|\\
0@>{}>{}>\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{m}@>{}>{}>\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{n+m}@>{}>{}>\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{n}@>{}>{}>0.\\
\end{CD}

This induces a commutative diagram of long exact sequences (written vertically),

\begin{CD}\text{CH}_{i}(X,j,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})@>{\mod\ell^{m}}>{}>\text{CH}_{i}(X,j,\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{m})@>{cl_{i,j}}>{}>H_{2i+j}^{\text{BM}}(X,\mathbb{Z}/{\ell^{m}}(i))\\
@V{}V{\times\ell^{n}}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
\text{CH}_{i}(X,j,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})@>{\mod\ell^{n+m}}>{}>\text{CH}_{i}(X,j,\mathbb{Z}/{\ell^{n+m}})@>{cl_{i,j}}>{}>H_{2i+j}^{\text{BM}}(X,\mathbb{Z}/{\ell^{n+m}}(i))\\
@V{}V{\mod\ell^{n}}V@V{}V{\mod\ell^{n}}V@V{}V{\mod\ell^{n}}V\\
\text{CH}_{i}(X,j,\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{n})=\text{CH}_{i}(X,j,\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{n})@>{cl_{i,j}}>{}>H_{2i+j}^{\text{BM}}(X,\mathbb{Z}/{\ell^{n}}(i))\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
\text{CH}_{i}(X,j-1,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})@>{\mod\ell^{m}}>{}>\text{CH}_{i}(X,j-1,\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{m})@>{cl_{i,j-1}}>{}>H_{2i+j-1}^{\text{BM}}(X,\mathbb{Z}/{\ell^{m}}(i))\\
@V{}V{\times\ell^{n}}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
\text{CH}_{i}(X,j-1,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})@>{\mod\ell^{n+m}}>{}>\text{CH}_{i}(X,j-1,\mathbb{Z}/{\ell^{n+m}})@>{cl_{i,j-1}}>{}>H_{2i+j-1}^{\text{BM}}(X,\mathbb{Z}/{\ell^{n+m}}(i)),\end{CD}

where the first two columns are long exact sequences of higher Chow groups constructed in Lemma
2.20
, and the last column is the Bockstein long exact sequence of Borel-Moore homology induced by the exact sequence
0\to\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{m}\to\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{n+m}\xrightarrow{\mod\ell^{n}}\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{n}\to 0
.

Then we have a commutative diagram after taking the inverse limit with respect to
m
.

\begin{CD}\text{CH}_{i}(X,j,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})@>{}>{}>\lim\limits_{\xleftarrow[m]{}}\text{CH}_{i}(X,j,\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{m})@>{cl_{i,j}}>{}>H_{2i+j}^{\text{BM}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(i))\\
@V{}V{\times\ell^{n}}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{\times\ell^{n}}V\\
\text{CH}_{i}(X,j,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})@>{}>{}>\lim\limits_{\xleftarrow[m]{}}\text{CH}_{i}(X,j,\mathbb{Z}/{\ell^{n+m}})@>{cl_{i,j}}>{}>H_{2i+j}^{\text{BM}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(i))\\
@V{}V{\mod\ell^{n}}V@V{}V{\mod\ell^{n}}V@V{}V{\mod\ell^{n}}V\\
\text{CH}_{i}(X,j,\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{n})=\text{CH}_{i}(X,j,\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{n})@>{cl_{i,j}}>{}>H_{2i+j}^{\text{BM}}(X,\mathbb{Z}/{\ell^{n}}(i))\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
\text{CH}_{i}(X,j-1,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})@>{}>{}>\lim\limits_{\xleftarrow[m]{}}\text{CH}_{i}(X,j-1,\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{m})@>{cl_{i,j-1}}>{}>H_{2i+j-1}^{\text{BM}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(i))\\
@V{}V{\times\ell^{n}}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{\times\ell^{n}}V\\
\text{CH}_{i}(X,j-1,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})@>{}>{}>\lim\limits_{\xleftarrow[m]{}}\text{CH}_{i}(X,j-1,\mathbb{Z}/{\ell^{n+m}})@>{cl_{i,j-1}}>{}>H_{2i+j-1}^{\text{BM}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(i)).\end{CD}

The right column is still a long exact sequence since every Borel-Moore homology group is finite and the Mittag-Leffler condition is satisfied.
This proves the desired statement.
∎

2.4.
Various filtrations

This section studies several different notions of filtrations on the cohomology of a variety introduced by Benoist-Ottem and Voisin in
[
BO21
,
Voi22
]
.

Convention 2.22
.

We use the following conventions for cohomology and homology in this section.
Let
X
be a smooth projective variety of dimension
d
defined over an algebraically closed field
k
.
Given an abelian group
A
that is one of
\mathbb{Z}/m,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell},\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
, where
m
is a positive integer,
\ell
a prime number, both of which are relatively prime to the characteristic of
k
, we simply write
H^{k}(X,A)
as the étale cohomology with coefficient
A
(resp. the singular cohomology with coefficient
A
, if
X
is a complex variety), and
H_{i}(*,A)
as the étale Borel-Moore homology (resp. singular Borel-Moore homology).

Definition 2.23
.

With conventions as in Convention
2.22
, we define the following filtrations on the cohomology
H^{k}(X,A)
.

(1)

The coniveau filtration
\{N^{*}H^{k}(X,A)\}
with

N^{c}H^{k}(X,A):=\sum_{f:Y\to X}f_{*}(H_{2d-k}(Y,A))\subset H_{2d-k}(X,A)\cong H^{k}(X,A),

where the sum is taken over all morphisms from projective algebraic sets
f:Y\to X,\dim Y\leq d-c
;

(2)

The strong coniveau filtration
\{\tilde{N}^{*}H^{k}(X,A)\}
with

\tilde{N}^{c}H^{k}(X,A):=\sum_{f:Y\to X}f_{*}(H_{2d-k}(Y,A))\subset H_{2d-k}(X,A)\cong H^{k}(X,A),

where the sum is taken over all morphisms from
smooth
projective varieties
f:Y\to X,\dim Y\leq d-c
.

(3)

The strong cylindrical filtration
\{\tilde{N}_{*,\text{cyl}}H^{k}(X,A)\}
with

\tilde{N}_{c,\text{cyl}}H^{k}(X,A):=\sum\Gamma_{*}(H_{2d-k-2c}(Z,A))\subset H_{2d-k}(X,A)\cong H^{k}(X,A)

where the sum is taken over all
smooth
projective varieties
Z
and subvarieties
\Gamma\subset Z\times X
such that
\Gamma\to Z
is dominant of relative dimension
c
.

(4)

We use the notations
N^{c}H_{k}
etc. to denote the filtrations on Borel-Moore or singular homology
H_{k}
. Since
X
is smooth, this is the same as the filtrations
N^{c}H^{2d-k}
.

Definition 2.24
.

Let
X,Y,Z
be smooth projective varieties, and let
p:Y\to Z,q:Y\to X
be two morphisms. We define the correspondence action
Y_{*}
on higher Chow groups and cohomology as

Y_{*}=q_{*}\circ p^{*}.

Lemma 2.25
.

Keep the same notations as in Definition
2.24
. If
Y\xrightarrow{p\times q}Z\times X
is generically finite of degree
d
onto its image, then we have the following formula:

(6)

Y_{*}=d\cdot\Gamma_{Y*},

where
\Gamma_{Y}\in\text{CH}_{\dim Y}(Z\times X)
is the cycle class of the image of
Y
.

Consequently, if
Y
is birational to its image, or if we consider higher Chow groups or cohomology with coefficients in which
d
is invertible, the image of
Y_{*}
is the same as
\Gamma_{Y*}
.

Proof.

Denote by
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}
the projection of
Z\times X
to
Z
and
X
.
By the push-pull formula for higher Chow groups and cohomology, for any element
\alpha
of the higher Chow group or cohomology of
Z
, we have

\pi_{1}^{*}\alpha\cup(d\cdot\Gamma_{Y})=\pi_{1}^{*}\alpha\cup(p\times q)_{*}([Y])=(p\times q)_{*}((p\times q)^{*}(\pi_{1}^{*}\alpha))=(p\times q)_{*}(p^{*}\alpha).

Applying
\pi_{2*}
to the above equality proves Formula (
6
). The last statement is clear by the formula.
∎

We note that the strong cylindrical filtration is indeed a filtration.

Lemma 2.26
.

Keep the same notations as in Convention
2.22
.
We have

\tilde{N}_{c+1,\text{cyl}}H^{k}(X,A)\subset\tilde{N}_{c,\text{cyl}}H^{k}(X,A),c\geq 1.

Proof.

Note that the statement for
\mathbb{Z}/m
follows from that for
\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{r}
by considering prime factors of
m
separately. So we only consider étale cohomology with
\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{r}
,
\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}
, or
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
coefficients, or singular cohomology with
\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{Q}
coefficients (over complex numbers) in the following.

We may find a blow-up
\Gamma_{1}
of
\Gamma
, with morphisms
\Gamma_{1}\to Y\to Z
, where
Y\to Z
has relative dimension
1
and
\Gamma_{1}\to Y
has relative dimension
c
.

Using resolution of singularities (in case
A=\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{Q}
) or Gabber’s refinement of de Jong’s alteration (in other cases), we may find smooth projective varieties
\Gamma^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime}
, with a commutative diagram of varieties over
Z

\begin{CD}\Gamma^{\prime}@>{}>{}>Y^{\prime}\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
\Gamma_{1}@>{}>{}>Y,\end{CD}

where
\Gamma^{\prime}\to\Gamma_{1}
and
Y^{\prime}\to Y
are birational (in case
A=\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{Q}
) or generically finite with degree relatively prime to
\ell
(in other cases).

By Lemma
2.25
, as a correspondence from
Y
to
X
,
\Gamma_{*}
has the same image as
\Gamma_{*}^{\prime}
, which is the composition of first pulling back to
\Gamma^{\prime}
and then pushing forward to
X
.
So the image is contained in
\Gamma_{*}^{\prime}(H_{2d-k-2c}(Y^{\prime}))
for the correspondence given by the morphism
\Gamma^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}\times X
, which lies in
\tilde{N}_{c,\text{cyl}}H^{k}(X,A)
.
∎

Corollary 2.27
.

Keep the same notations as in Convention
2.22
.
Let
Z
be a smooth projective variety,
\Gamma\subset Z\times X
a subvariety of dimension
\dim Z+c(c\geq 1)
, and
W\subset Z
the image of
\Gamma
under the projection to
Z
. Assume that
\Gamma
is of relative dimension
c+r
over
W
. Then

\Gamma_{*}(H_{2d-k-2c}(Z,A))\subset\tilde{N}_{c+r,\text{cyl}}H^{k}(X,A)\subset\tilde{N}_{c,\text{cyl}}H^{k}(X,A).

As a corollary,

\tilde{N}_{c,\text{cyl}}H^{k}(X,A)=\sum_{\Gamma\in\text{CH}_{\dim Z+c}(Z\times X)}\Gamma_{*}(H_{2d-k-2c}(Z,A)).

Proof.

The case for
A=\mathbb{Z}/m
follows from
A=\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{n}
. So we assume that
A
is one of the following:
\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{n},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell},\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Q}
, or
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
.

We take either a resolution of singularities
W^{\prime}\to W
(if we work over the complex numbers and
A=\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{Q}
) or an alteration of degree relatively prime to
\ell
(in other cases) and find a smooth projective variety
\Gamma^{\prime}
with morphisms
\Gamma^{\prime}\to W^{\prime}\times X
such that
\Gamma^{\prime}\to W\times X
has image equal to
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}\to\Gamma
is either birational (if we work over the complex numbers and
A=\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{Q}
) or generically finite of degree relatively prime to
\ell
(in other cases).
We see that

\displaystyle\Gamma_{*}(H_{2d-k-2c}(Z))=\Gamma_{*}^{\prime}(H_{2d-k-2c}(Z))\subset\Gamma_{*}^{\prime}((H_{2d-k-2c-2r}(W^{\prime})))

\displaystyle\subset

\displaystyle\tilde{N}_{c+r,\text{cyl}}H^{k}(X)\subset\tilde{N}_{c,\text{cyl}}H^{k}(X),

where the first equality is due to Lemma
2.25
, and the last inclusion follows from Lemma
2.27
.
∎

Strong cylindrical filtrations are generated by a restricted class of correspondences.
To see this, we need the following lemma, which will be used several times.

Lemma 2.28

(The Flattening Lemma)
.

[
RG71
, Page 37]
,
[
dJ96
, 2.19]

Let
f:X\to S
be a projective, dominant morphism between noetherian schemes.
Assume that
S
is integral.
There exists a birational projective morphism
S^{\prime}\to S
such that the strict transform
f^{\prime}:X^{\prime}\to S^{\prime}
is flat.

Note that the cited reference
[
dJ96
]
does not explicitly state the projectivity of the birational morphism
S^{\prime}\to S
.
But this can be deduced from the proof.
See the first two paragraphs of page 37 in
[
RG71
]
.

Lemma 2.29
.

Keep the same notations as in Convention
2.22
. We have

\tilde{N}_{c,\text{cyl}}H^{k}(X,A)=\sum_{\Gamma\subset Z\times X}\Gamma_{*}(H_{2d-k-2c}(Z,A)),c\geq 1

where the sum is taken over all smooth projective varieties
Z
and all subvarieties
\Gamma\subset Z\times X
such that
\Gamma\to Z
is flat (or has equi-dimensional fibers).

Proof.

As before, we only need to consider
A=\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell},\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{n},\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
. Also we only need to show that the left hand side is contained in the right hand side.

Consider a subvariety
\Gamma\subset Z\times X
such that
\Gamma\to Z
has relative dimension
c
.
By the flattening lemma
2.28
, there is a commutative diagram:

\begin{CD}\Gamma_{1}@>{}>{}>\Gamma\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
Z_{1}@>{}>{}>Z,\end{CD}

where
Z_{1}\to Z
and
\Gamma_{1}\to\Gamma
are birational, and the morphism
\Gamma_{1}\to Z_{1}
is flat.
We then use resolution of singularities in characteristic
0
or Gabber’s refinement of de Jong’s alteration and extend the previous commutative diagram:

\begin{CD}\Gamma_{2}@>{}>{}>\Gamma_{1}@>{}>{}>\Gamma\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
Z_{2}@>{}>{}>Z_{1}@>{}>{}>Z,\end{CD}

where
Z_{2}\to Z_{1}
is a resolution of singularities (in the case
A=\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{Q}
) or an alteration of degree relatively prime to
\ell
(in other cases), and the left square is Cartesian.
Note that by construction,

\Gamma_{1}\subset Z_{1}\times X,\Gamma_{2}\subset Z_{2}\times X,

are closed subschemes. In particular, we may take their fundamental classes in the Chow group and get correspondences.
Finally take an alteration
\Gamma_{3}\to\Gamma_{2}
of degree relatively prime to
\ell
.
Then by Lemma
2.25

\displaystyle\Gamma_{*}(H_{2d-c-k}(Z,A))=\Gamma_{3*}(H_{2d-c-k}(Z,A))

\displaystyle\subset

\displaystyle\Gamma_{3*}(H_{2d-c-k}(Z_{2},A))=\Gamma_{2*}(H_{2d-c-k}(Z_{2},A)).

∎

A different way of defining the strong coniveau filtration is the following.

Lemma 2.30
.

Keep the same notations as in Convention
2.22
. We have an equality

\tilde{N}^{c}H^{k}(X,A)=\sum_{\Gamma\in\text{CH}_{\dim Z}(Z\times X),\dim Z\leq\dim X-c}\Gamma_{*}(H_{2\dim X-k}(Z,A)),c\geq 1.

When
A=\mathbb{Z}
, this is
[
BO21
, Equation 2.1]
. The general case only needs to replace the resolution of singularities in their argument by Gabber’s refinement of de Jong’s alteration.

Proof.

The left hand side is contained in the right hand side since we may take
\Gamma
to be the graph of
Z\to X
.
To see the reverse inclusion, we may assume that
\Gamma
is represented by an integral subvariety of
Z\times X
. Take a resolution of singularities
\Gamma^{\prime}\to\Gamma
(in case
A=\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{Q}
) or an alteration
\Gamma^{\prime}\to\Gamma
whose degree is relatively prime to
\ell
(in other cases).
Then by Lemma
2.25
,
\Gamma_{*}(H_{2\dim X-k}(Z,A))
is contained in the push-forward of
H_{2\dim X-k}(\Gamma^{\prime},A)
via the morphism
\Gamma^{\prime}\to X
.
∎

Corollary 2.31
.

Keep the same notations as in Convention
2.22
.
Let

\Gamma\in\text{CH}_{\dim X}(X\times Y)

be a correspondence between smooth projective varieties
X,Y
. Assume that
\dim Y=\dim X+r
. Then for any
c\geq 1
,

\Gamma_{*}(\tilde{N}^{c}H^{k}(X,A))\subset\tilde{N}^{c+r}H^{2r+k}(Y,A),\quad\Gamma_{*}(\tilde{N}_{c,\text{cyl}}H^{k}(X,A))\subset\tilde{N}_{c,\text{cyl}}H^{2r+k}(Y,A).

Proof.

By Lemma
2.30
,
\tilde{N}^{c}H^{k}(X,A)
is generated by
\gamma_{*}(H_{2\dim X-k}(Z,A))
for all
\gamma\in\text{CH}_{\dim Z}(Z\times X),\dim Z\leq\dim X-c
.
Then

\Gamma_{*}(\gamma_{*}(H_{2\dim X-k}(Z,A)))=(\Gamma\circ\gamma)_{*}(H_{2\dim X-k}(Z,A)),

where
\Gamma\circ\gamma\in\text{CH}_{\dim Z}(Z\times Y)
is the composition of the correspondences
\Gamma
and
\gamma
.

The proof for strong cylindrical filtrations is the same by Corollary
2.27
.
∎

Lemma 2.32

(
[
SS23
, Lemma 2.3]
)
.

Keep the same notations as in Convention
2.22
. The action of any correspondence
\Gamma\in\text{CH}_{\dim X}(X\times Y)
on cohomology preserves the coniveau filtration.

Proof.

This is essentially
[
SS23
, Lemma 2.3]
, where the authors prove the statement for étale cohomology with
\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}
-coefficients. But the same proof works for other cohomology groups as well. The key point is that one can use a moving lemma to replace the correspondence by a rationally equivalent cycle that meets the support of the cohomology class properly.
∎

A very important observation, first made in
[
Voi22
]
for complex varieties with integral singular cohomology, is the following.

Lemma 2.33
.

[
Voi22
, Proposition 1.3]

[
SS23
, Lemma 2.4]

Keep the same notations as in Convention
2.22
.
For
A=\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}
and
X
of dimension
d
, we have the following equality:

\tilde{N}_{d-c,\text{cyl}}H^{2c-1}(X,A)=\tilde{N}^{c-1}H^{2c-1}(X,A),c\geq 1.

A natural question is whether or not the filtrations in Definition
2.23
agree with each other.
If we use
\mathbb{Q}
or
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
coefficients, the theory of weights shows that the strong coniveau and coniveau filtrations are the same.
Since the difference between some of these filtrations also gives stable birational invariants, one wonders if this could be used to prove non-stable-rationality for some rationally connected varieties.

Examples over
\mathbb{C}
with
\mathbb{Z}
-coefficients where the strong coniveau filtration and coniveau filtration differ are constructed in
[
BO21
]
for every possible
c,k
(coniveau
c
on degree
k
cohomology). Examples in positive characteristic are constructed by Scavia-Suzuki
[
SS22
]
and Benoist
[
Ben22
]
.
These examples all have dimension at least
4
.

Voisin studied the (strong) coniveau filtrations on
H^{2d-3}

[
Voi22
]
on complex projective manifolds.
For the definition of
N_{1,\text{cyl},\text{st}}H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z})
, see
[
Voi22
, Definition 1.12]
.

Theorem 2.34
.

[
Voi22
, Theorem 2.6, Corollary 2.7, Theorem 2.17]

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety of dimension
d
defined over
\mathbb{C}
.

(1)

Assume the Walker Abel-Jacobi map (
[
Wal07
]
)

\phi:\text{CH}_{1}(X)_{\text{alg}}\to J(N^{d-2}H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z}))

is injective on torsion. Then we have

N_{1,\text{cyl},\text{st}}H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z})/\text{Tor}=N^{d-2}H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z})/\text{Tor}.

(2)

If
\dim X
is
3
, we have

N_{1,\text{cyl},\text{st}}H^{3}(X,\mathbb{Z})/\text{Tor}=N^{1}H^{3}(X,\mathbb{Z})/\text{Tor}.

(3)

If
X
is rationally connected, we have

N_{1,\text{cyl},\text{st}}H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z})=\tilde{N}^{d-2}H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z}).

As a consequence,

N_{1,\text{cyl},\text{st}}H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z})/\text{Tor}=\tilde{N}^{d-2}H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z})/\text{Tor}=N^{d-2}H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z})/\text{Tor}.

We will prove an improvement of Voisin’s result in Section
4
and the
\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}
-counterpart in Section
5
, which include all the torsion classes.

Finally we note the following.

Lemma 2.35
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety of dimension
d
defined over an algebraically closed field
k
, whose rational Chow group of zero-cycles is universally supported on a surface.
Fix
\ell
a prime number distinct from the characteristic of
k
.
Then the quotients

H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})/N^{d-2}H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}),\quad H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})/\tilde{N}^{d-2}H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})

are torsion.
In particular, this is true for smooth projective varieties that are separably rationally connected in codimension one.

Proof.

Since we have inclusions

\tilde{N}^{d-2}H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})\subset{N}^{d-2}H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})\subset H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}),

it suffices to show that
H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})/\tilde{N}^{d-2}H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})
is torsion.

By Lemma
2.5
, there is a decomposition of diagonal of the form

N\Delta_{X}=\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2}\in\text{CH}_{\dim X}(X\times X),

where
N
is an integer,
\Gamma_{1}
is a cycle supported in
X\times Z
for some
2
-dimensional closed subset
Z
, and
\Gamma_{2}
is a cycle supported in
D\times X
for some divisor
D\subset X
.

By Corollary
2.31
, correspondences preserve the strong coniveau filtration. So it suffices to show that
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
acts by zero on the quotient

H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})/\tilde{N}^{d-2}H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}).

Since the correspondence action is linear, we may assume that
\Gamma_{1}
is the class of an irreducible subvariety of
X\times X
, still denoted by
\Gamma_{1}
, and that
Z
is also irreducible.
Take alterations
\Gamma_{1}^{\prime},Z^{\prime}
of
\Gamma_{1}
and
Z
of degree relatively prime to
\ell
such that there is a commutative diagram

\begin{CD}\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}@>{}>{}>\Gamma_{1}\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
Z^{\prime}@>{}>{}>Z.\end{CD}

Denote by
f:Z^{\prime}\to Z\subset X
the morphism to
X
.
Then by Lemma
2.25
,

\Gamma_{1*}(H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}))=\Gamma_{1*}^{\prime}(H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}))\subset f_{*}(H_{3}(Z^{\prime},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}))\subset\tilde{N}^{d-2}H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}).

So
\Gamma_{1*}
acts by zero on
H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})/\tilde{N}^{d-2}H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})
.

By Corollary
2.27
and Lemma
2.33
,

\Gamma_{2*}(H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}))\subset\tilde{N}_{1,\text{cyl}}H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})=\tilde{N}^{d-2}H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}).

Thus
\Gamma_{2*}
also acts by zero on
H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})/\tilde{N}^{d-2}H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})
.
∎

2.5.
Some stable birational invariants

We include the proof of the following.

Lemma 2.36
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety of dimension
d
defined over an algebraically closed field. Fix a prime number
\ell
different from the field characteristic. All cohomology groups considered are étale cohomology groups. The following invariants are stable birational invariants of
X
.

(1)

The kernel and cokernel of the cycle class map

A_{1}(X)\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}\to H^{2d-2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(d-1)).

(2)

The kernel and cokernel of the cycle class maps

(7)

\lim\limits_{\xleftarrow[n]{}}\text{CH}_{1}({X},r,\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{n}\mathbb{Z})\to H^{2d-2-r}({X},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(d-1)).

(3)

The quotients

H_{i}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})/\tilde{N}_{1,\text{cyl}}H_{i}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})=H^{2d-i}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})/\tilde{N}_{1,\text{cyl}}H^{2d-i}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}),

H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})/\tilde{N}^{d-2}H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}),

and

H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})/{N}^{d-2}H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}).

A word of caution is in order. These invariants are homological and covariant for proper morphisms.
One should be particularly careful when comparing the invariants
H^{2d-i}(*,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})/\tilde{N}_{1,\text{cyl}}H^{2d-i}(*,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})
for
X\times\mathbb{P}^{n}
and
X
. The invariance means that the push-forward (instead of pull-back) along the first factor induces an isomorphism between
H^{2d-i+2n}(X\times\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})/\tilde{N}_{1,\text{cyl}}H^{2d-i+2n}(X\times\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})
and
H^{2d-i}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})/\tilde{N}_{1,\text{cyl}}H^{2d-i}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})
.

This lemma is well-known to experts. Over the complex numbers with singular cohomology, the first statement can be found in
[
Voi16
]
as Lemma 2.22 and Lemma 2.23, and the third in
[
Voi22
, Proposition 1.4]
. The proofs in the references use the weak factorization theorem to reduce to a smooth blow-up case, and thus do not immediately apply in positive characteristic.
The following proof using correspondences is also well-known to experts.

Proof.

We first prove that these are birational invariants.

Let
Y
be a smooth projective variety birational to
X
, with birational maps
f:X\dashrightarrow Y,g:Y\dashrightarrow X
that are inverse to each other. Denote by
\Gamma_{f}\subset X\times Y,\Gamma_{g}\subset Y\times X
the closure of the graph of
f,g
. Finally, denote by
\Gamma_{X}=\Gamma_{g}\circ\Gamma_{f}
the composition of correspondences.

First observe that any correspondence
\Gamma\in\text{CH}_{\dim X}(X\times Y)
acts on higher Chow groups and cohomology, and preserves
\tilde{N}_{1,\text{cyl}}H^{i}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})
(Corollary
2.31
) and
{N}^{d-2}H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})
(Lemma
2.32
).
Moreover, the action on higher Chow groups and cohomology is compatible with cycle class maps by Proposition
2.19
.
Therefore, correspondences act on all the invariants that we consider.

So it suffices to show that
\Gamma_{X}
acts by identity on the invariants. Since then by symmetry,
\Gamma_{Y}=\Gamma_{f}\circ\Gamma_{g}
also acts by identity, and thus both
\Gamma_{f}
and
\Gamma_{g}
are isomorphisms.

Since
f,g
are inverse birational maps, there is an open subset
U\subset X
such that
g\circ f:U\to X
is the inclusion. Therefore, when restricted to
U\times X
, the cycle
\Gamma_{X}
is the same as the graph of the inclusion of
U\subset X
. By the localization sequence for Chow groups, we know that

\Gamma_{X}=\Delta_{X}+Z\in\text{CH}_{\dim X}(X\times X)

for some cycle
Z
supported in
D\times X
, where
\Delta_{X}
is the diagonal, and
D\subset X
is a divisor.
Since
\Delta_{X}
acts as identity, we only need to show that
Z
acts as the zero map on the invariants.

Since the correspondence action is linear, we may assume that
Z
and
D
are irreducible. By Gabber’s refinement of de Jong’s alteration, there are smooth projective varieties
Z^{\prime},D^{\prime}
with the following commutative diagram

\begin{CD}Z^{\prime}@>{}>{}>Z\\
@V{p}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
D^{\prime}@>{}>{}>D\\
\end{CD}

such that
Z^{\prime}\to Z
and
D^{\prime}\to D
are generically finite with degrees relatively prime to
\ell
.

By Lemma
2.25
, up to an invertible factor, the action of
Z
on
A_{1}(X)\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}
agrees with

A_{1}(X)\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}\to A_{0}(D^{\prime})\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}\xrightarrow{p^{*}}A_{1}(Z^{\prime})\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}\to A_{1}(X)\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{\ell},

where the first two maps are pull-backs and the last one is push-forward.
Similarly, up to an invertible factor, the action on
H^{2d-2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})
agrees with

H^{2d-2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})\to H^{2d-2}(D^{\prime},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})\to H^{2d-2}(Z^{\prime},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})\to H^{2d-2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}),

where the first two maps are pull-backs and the last one is Gysin push-forward.
Moreover, there is an obvious commutative diagram of these maps.
In particular, up to multiplying by an integer relatively prime to
\ell
, the action of
Z
on the kernel (resp. cokernel) of

A_{1}(X)\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}\to H^{2d-2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(d-1)),

factors through the kernel (resp. cokernel) of

A_{0}(D^{\prime})\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}\xrightarrow{cl}H^{2d-2}(D^{\prime},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(d-1)),

Since the cycle class map for zero-cycles on
D^{\prime}
modulo algebraic equivalence induces
an isomorphism in the above cycle class map to cohomology, we see that
Z
acts as zero.

The case for the kernel and cokernel of the cycle class map (
7
) is similar. We use the fact that for
D^{\prime}
, the higher cycle class map induces an isomorphism (Corollary
2.17
).

CH_{0}(D^{\prime},r,\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{n}\mathbb{Z})\xrightarrow[\cong]{cl_{0,j}}H^{2d-2-r}(D^{\prime},\mathbb{Z}/{\ell^{n}}(d-1))

and hence we have an isomorphism after taking inverse limit.

For the third statement, notice that
Z_{*}(H_{i}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}))

lies in the
\tilde{N}_{1,\text{cyl}}H_{i}
part of the strong cylindrical filtration by Corollary
2.27
. Thus
Z
acts by zero on
H_{i}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})/\tilde{N}_{1,\text{cyl}}H_{i}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})
.

Finally, by Lemma
2.33
, and the fact that
Z_{*}(H_{i}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}))

lies in the
\tilde{N}_{1,\text{cyl}}H_{i}
part of the strong cylindrical filtration by Corollary
2.27
, the correspondence
Z
acts as
0
on
H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})/\tilde{N}^{2d-3}H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}),H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})/{N}^{2d-3}H^{2d-3}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})
.

So far we have shown that these invariants are birational invariants.
To prove that these are stable birational invariants, it then suffices to show that for
X
and
X\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, these invariants are the same.

The first two statements follow from the projective bundle formulae for higher Chow groups and étale cohomology. Indeed, we have

H^{i}(X\times\mathbb{P}^{1})=H^{i-2}(X)\cdot h\oplus H^{i}(X)\cdot 1,

A_{1}(X\times\mathbb{P}^{1})=A_{1}(X)\cdot h\oplus A_{0}(X)\cdot 1,

\text{CH}_{1}(X\times\mathbb{P}^{1},r,\mathbb{Z}/m)=\text{CH}_{1}(X,r,\mathbb{Z}/m)\cdot h\oplus CH_{0}(X,r,\mathbb{Z}/m)\cdot 1,

where
h
denotes the class of a hyperplane in cohomology or Chow group.
In the case of cohomology, the same type of formulae holds for torsion coefficients and
\ell
-adic cohomology. We also use the fact (Corollary
2.17
) that

CH_{0}(X,r,\mathbb{Z}/m)\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{2d-r}(X,\mathbb{Z}/m),

and

A_{0}(X)\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{2d}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}).

For the various filtrations, simply note that the subgroup
H^{i}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})\otimes 1
is in
\tilde{N}_{1,\text{cyl}}H^{i}(X\times\mathbb{P}^{1},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})
, since it lies in the image of

H^{i}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})\xrightarrow{\pi_{X}^{*}}H^{i}(X\times\mathbb{P}^{1},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})\xrightarrow{id_{*}}H^{i}(X\times\mathbb{P}^{1},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}).

where we consider
X\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
as a correspondence between
X
and
X\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
via the projection to the first factor and the identity map.
∎

3.
Space of one-cycles

In this section, we fix an algebraically closed field
k
of any characteristic.
We remind the readers that a variety is always assumed to be irreducible, and in particular, connected, throughout the paper.
Sometimes we add the word irreducible just to emphasize this.

3.1.
Setup and statements

Definition 3.1

(
[
SV00b
, Definition 3.1.3, Lemma 3.3.9]
)
.

Let
X,S
be finite type reduced separated
k
-schemes. An equi-dimensional family of relative cycles of dimension
r
(or simply
r
-cycles) over
S
is a formal linear combination of integral subschemes
\Gamma_{S}=\sum m_{i}Z_{i},Z_{i}\subset S\times X,m_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
, such that

(1)

Each
Z_{i}
dominates one irreducible component of
S

(2)

Each non-empty fiber of
Z_{i}\to S
has pure dimension
r
.

(3)

A fat point condition is satisfied (
[
SV00b
, Definition 3.1.3]
).

(4)

A field of definition condition is satisfied (
[
SV00b
, Lemma 3.3.9 and the ensuing paragraph]
).

Each
Z_{i}
is called a
component
of
\Gamma_{S}
.
We write
\Gamma_{S}^{+}
and
\Gamma_{S}^{-}
as the
positive and negative parts
, i.e. the sum of
Z_{i}
’s with positive and negative coefficients.

Suslin and Voevodsky consider a more general setup in
[
SV00b
]
: a finite type scheme
X
over a noetherian scheme
S
, which could be non-reduced. We only consider families of cycles over a reduced base. To apply their definitions and results to our situation, one considers
X\times S\to S
.

Remark 3.2
.

For a normal variety
S
(or more generally, a geometrically unibranch variety), condition
3
is automatic
[
SV00b
, Corollary 3.4.3, 3.4.4]
. Condition
4
is always satisfied if the base is regular
[
SV00b
, Corollary 3.4.5, 3.4.6]
.

We refer the interested readers to
[
SV00b
]
Section 3 for details about the last two conditions in Definition
3.1
and the subtle points about these conditions. We only remark that with this definition, one can pull-back families of cycles, that is, we have a presheaf on the category of schemes (
[
SV00b
, paragraph before Lemma 3.3.10]
).

Notation 3.3
.

Given an equi-dimensional family of cycles
\Gamma_{S}\to S
and a morphism
f:T\to S
, we denote by
\Gamma_{T}=f^{*}\Gamma_{S}\to T
, the pull-back family of cycles over
T
.
If
f
is a locally closed immersion, we also use the notation
\Gamma_{T}=\Gamma_{S}|_{T}
to denote the pull-back family.

The following is well-known.

Lemma 3.4
.

Given a flat projective family of curves
C\to S
and an
S
-morphism
F:C\to S\times X
, we can associate to it an equi-dimensional family of one-cycles of
X
over
S
.

Proof.

We take the graph
C\subset C\times_{S}(S\times X)\cong C\times X
.
The subscheme
C\subset C\times X
is flat and proper over
S
. Applying
[
SV00b
, Corollary 3.2.5]
, we get a family of one-dimensional cycles in
C\times X/S
over the base
S
. Then pushing-forward via the projection to
S\times X
gives an equi-dimensional family of one-cycles of
X
over
S
(
[
SV00b
, Corollary 3.6.3]
).
∎

Definition 3.5
.

A nodal family of one-cycles over
S
, denoted by
S\leftarrow\Gamma_{S}\to X,\Gamma_{S}=\sum m_{i}Z_{i}(m_{i}\in\mathbb{Z})
, is a finite
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combination of isomorphism classes of
S\leftarrow Z_{i}\to X
, where
S\leftarrow Z_{i}\to X
is a flat projective family of connected nodal curves over
S
with a morphism to
X
.
If
S=\text{Spec }k
, we also call
\Gamma_{S}
a
nodal one-cycle
.

Each
Z_{i}
is called a
component
of
\Gamma_{S}
.
We use
[\Gamma_{S}]\to S
to denote the associated equi-dimensional family of one-cycles over
S
as in Lemma
3.4
.

We write
\Gamma_{S}^{+}
and
\Gamma_{S}^{-}
as the
positive and negative parts
, i.e. the sum of
Z_{i}
’s with positive and negative coefficients.
We say that
\Gamma_{S}
is
effective
if
\Gamma_{S}^{-}
is
0
.
